      <h2 id="outcome">A plugin that already knows your company</h2>
      <p class="lede">A configured Sales plugin starts from your company's facts and works your way. Each stock skill lists the company facts it needs and looks for them in your connected tools and instructions. When a fact is missing, the skill infers it from what it can read or uses a labeled default, and asks one question where the answer would change the result. It keeps that answer for the conversation only. Configuration writes those answers down once.</p>

      <div class="scroll"><table class="stack t-cmp"><thead><tr><th scope="col">You ask</th><th scope="col">Stock plugin</th><th scope="col">Configured plugin</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td data-l="You ask">Triage this inbound lead</td><td data-l="Stock plugin">With no ideal customer written down, scores on intent alone. Asks about it only if the answer would change the priority.</td><td data-l="Configured plugin">Scores against your written ICP and disqualifiers, and names your handoff channel.</td></tr><tr><td data-l="You ask">Prep me for tomorrow's call</td><td data-l="Stock plugin">Uses your CRM's stage names, and asks which qualification method you run.</td><td data-l="Configured plugin">Builds discovery questions around your qualification method.</td></tr><tr><td data-l="You ask">Draft a follow-up</td><td data-l="Stock plugin">Writes in your voice if it has learned it, then by a style guide you uploaded or your sent mail where it can read it, otherwise in a neutral one. Knows no company wording rules.</td><td data-l="Configured plugin">Follows your messaging rules, approved proof points and signature.</td></tr><tr><td data-l="You ask">Write my forecast</td><td data-l="Stock plugin">Uses the CRM's own forecast category where it has one. Otherwise infers the categories from your stages, and asks which stages count as commit only if the answer depends on it.</td><td data-l="Configured plugin">Uses your mapping of stages to forecast categories, where the CRM has no forecast category of its own.</td></tr><tr><td data-l="You ask">Which renewals are coming up?</td><td data-l="Stock plugin">Reads where renewal dates live from the CRM and from what it already knows about your company, and asks once only if neither tells it.</td><td data-l="Configured plugin">Reads the field you named.</td></tr></tbody></table></div>
<p class="note">This comparison comes from reading what each stock skill asks for. It is not a benchmark.</p>

      <h3>What configuration buys you</h3>
      <ul class="princ"><li><b>Fewer repeated questions</b>A stock answer lasts one conversation. A configured fact is there every time, for every skill that needs it.</li><li><b>The same rules for everyone</b>Every seller's skills use one ICP, one set of stage definitions and one set of wording rules.</li><li><b>Guardrails that travel</b>A rule such as "no pricing by email" is given to every draft, whoever asks for it. It is an instruction to the model. A tool permission can block every send or hold each one for a yes. It cannot check what an email says, so a rule about content still needs a person's review.</li><li><b>Honest gaps</b>Where your company has not decided something, the configuration can say so. The skill then reports the gap instead of inventing a rule.</li></ul>
<h3>What it does not do</h3>
      <div class="box">
        <p>Configuration does not clean a messy CRM or settle a sales process you have not decided. It makes the plugin repeat your decisions, good or bad.</p>
        <p>This guide makes no claims about time saved or deals won. The benefits above are how the plugin is designed to behave. Measure the results on your own team before you quote them.</p>
      </div>
    

      <h2 id="role-setup">One setup, and what each role gets from it</h2>
      <p class="lede">One company configuration serves every role. Roles differ in which skills they use, and so in which facts matter most.</p>
      <p>This map is a suggestion drawn from the skill descriptions. The plugin itself assigns nothing, and no skill is locked to a role.</p>

      <div class="g2 rolecards"><div class="def rolecard" data-grole="sdr"><h3>SDR or BDR</h3><p>Inbound leads are scored against your written ICP and disqualifiers and matched to an owner by your rules. Once you accept the routing and ask for the post, the handoff goes to the channel you named, with the note you specified.</p><dl class="kv"><div><dt>Leans on</dt><dd>ICP and disqualifiers · Routing and handoff · Value proposition · Wording rules for outreach</dd></div><div><dt>Skills used most</dt><dd class="chips"><a class="chip k-P" href="#skill-lead-triage">lead-triage<span class="vh">, can share its output</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-route-lead">route-lead<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a><a class="chip k-R" href="#skill-account-research">account-research<span class="vh">, reads and proposes</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-draft-outreach">draft-outreach<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-schedule-meeting">schedule-meeting<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-log-activity">log-activity<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a></dd></div><div><dt>Configuration tip</dt><dd>Define a qualified lead, who receives it and what the handoff note must contain.</dd></div><div><dt>Written down, it looks like this</dt><dd class="eg">A lead is qualified when a director or above confirms the problem and agrees to a call. Hand off in the inbound channel with company, contact, trigger and next step.</dd></div></dl></div><div class="def rolecard" data-grole="ae"><h3>Account executive</h3><p>The deal skills check each deal against your stage exit criteria and your qualification method, and your forecast uses one mapping of stages to categories.</p><dl class="kv"><div><dt>Leans on</dt><dd>Stage exit criteria · Qualification method · Stakeholders and approvals · Competitors · Objections · Forecast categories</dd></div><div><dt>Skills used most</dt><dd class="chips"><a class="chip k-R" href="#skill-call-prep">call-prep<span class="vh">, reads and proposes</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-call-summary">call-summary<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-stakeholder-map">stakeholder-map<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a><a class="chip k-R" href="#skill-deal-review">deal-review<span class="vh">, reads and proposes</span></a><a class="chip k-R" href="#skill-deal-advance-gap">deal-advance-gap<span class="vh">, reads and proposes</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-close-plan">close-plan<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a><a class="chip k-C" href="#skill-handle-objection">handle-objection<span class="vh">, writes for customers</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-update-opportunity">update-opportunity<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-forecast">forecast<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a></dd></div><div><dt>Configuration tip</dt><dd>Write exit criteria for each stage. Four skills check deals against them directly: <a class="skl" href="#skill-deal-review"><code>deal-review</code></a>, <a class="skl" href="#skill-deal-advance-gap"><code>deal-advance-gap</code></a>, <a class="skl" href="#skill-crm-hygiene-check"><code>crm-hygiene-check</code></a> and <a class="skl" href="#skill-update-opportunity"><code>update-opportunity</code></a>. Others use them too.</dd></div><div><dt>Written down, it looks like this</dt><dd class="eg">A deal leaves Discovery only when the economic buyer is named and the next meeting is booked.</dd></div></dl></div><div class="def rolecard" data-grole="am"><h3>Account manager or CSM</h3><p>Renewal and health skills read the fields you named, so upcoming renewals come from your data instead of a question.</p><dl class="kv"><div><dt>Leans on</dt><dd>Renewal data location · Health and adoption fields · Product catalog · Where success metrics live</dd></div><div><dt>Skills used most</dt><dd class="chips"><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-customer-health">customer-health<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-renewal-radar">renewal-radar<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-expansion-whitespace">expansion-whitespace<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a><a class="chip k-R" href="#skill-customer-voice">customer-voice<span class="vh">, reads and proposes</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-account-plan">account-plan<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a><a class="chip k-P" href="#skill-account-context">account-context<span class="vh">, can share its output</span></a></dd></div><div><dt>Configuration tip</dt><dd>Name the renewal field first. The renewal skill calls that mapping "the whole game".</dd></div><div><dt>Written down, it looks like this</dt><dd class="eg">Renewal date is the Contract End Date field on the company record. Flag any renewal inside 120 days with no meeting booked.</dd></div></dl></div><div class="def rolecard" data-grole="lead"><h3>Sales leader</h3><p>Forecasts use one mapping of stages to categories, team pipeline reviews use one coverage target and win-loss reviews use one loss-reason field.</p><dl class="kv"><div><dt>Leans on</dt><dd>Forecast categories · Coverage target · Quota source · Loss-reason field · Team channels</dd></div><div><dt>Skills used most</dt><dd class="chips"><a class="chip k-P" href="#skill-team-pipeline">team-pipeline<span class="vh">, can share its output</span></a><a class="chip k-P" href="#skill-rep-context">rep-context<span class="vh">, can share its output</span></a><a class="chip k-R" href="#skill-pipeline-review">pipeline-review<span class="vh">, reads and proposes</span></a><a class="chip k-W" href="#skill-forecast">forecast<span class="vh">, changes records</span></a><a class="chip k-R" href="#skill-win-loss-review">win-loss-review<span class="vh">, reads and proposes</span></a><a class="chip k-R" href="#skill-deal-slip-scenario">deal-slip-scenario<span class="vh">, reads and proposes</span></a></dd></div><div><dt>Configuration tip</dt><dd>Fix the category mapping and coverage target once, so every forecast reads the same way.</dd></div><div><dt>Written down, it looks like this</dt><dd class="eg">Commit means Contract Sent. Best case means Proposal and Negotiation. The coverage target is 3 times quota.</dd></div></dl></div><div class="def rolecard" data-grole="ops"><h3>Revenue operations or enablement</h3><p>One reference page holds every fact, its source document and the open decisions.</p><dl class="kv"><div><dt>Leans on</dt><dd>All of the facts, plus connectors, permissions and distribution</dd></div><div><dt>Skills used most</dt><dd class="chips"><a class="chip k-S" href="#skill-setup">setup<span class="vh">, the first run</span></a><a class="chip k-R" href="#skill-crm-hygiene-check">crm-hygiene-check<span class="vh">, reads and proposes</span></a><span>and spot checks of the rest</span></dd></div><div><dt>Configuration tip</dt><dd>Own the reference page.</dd></div></dl></div><div class="def rolecard" data-grole="founder"><h3>Founder or full-cycle seller</h3><p>The facts you repeat most often are answered once, in every skill that needs them.</p><dl class="kv"><div><dt>Leans on</dt><dd>ICP · Messaging rules · Voice</dd></div><div><dt>Skills used most</dt><dd>The daily skills from every other role.</dd></div><div><dt>Configuration tip</dt><dd>Start with the five facts you repeat most often.</dd></div></dl></div></div>
<p class="note">The example lines are invented for a fictional company. They show the level of detail that works: one decision, stated plainly.</p>

      <h3>What the stock plugin already does by role</h3>
      <p>The plugin adapts to the person before you configure anything. Its <a class="skl" href="#skill-setup"><code>setup</code></a> skill infers a role from the CRM: AE, BDR or SDR, leader, or customer success. It then builds a role-aware dashboard and recommends three skills to start with. Three skills, <a class="skl" href="#skill-team-pipeline"><code>team-pipeline</code></a>, <a class="skl" href="#skill-rep-context"><code>rep-context</code></a> and <a class="skl" href="#skill-win-loss-review"><code>win-loss-review</code></a>, are written for leaders.</p>

      <h3>Where configuration differs by role</h3>
      <ul>
        <li><strong>Emphasis.</strong> Every role shares the same facts, but each stresses different ones. Configure first the rows for the roles you actually have.</li>
        <li><strong>Handoffs.</strong> Configuration pays most where work changes hands. Lead to AE needs routing rules and a handoff note. AE to account manager needs renewal dates and products recorded at close.</li>
        <li><strong>The personal layer.</strong> Voice, signature, book and territory belong to one person. Let <a class="skl" href="#skill-setup"><code>setup</code></a> learn them, and keep them in that person's own instructions, not the shared plugin.</li>
        <li><strong>Access.</strong> Owners on Team and Enterprise plans set tool permissions for the whole organization. Claude also acts with each person's own access in the CRM, so your CRM roles still apply.</li>
      </ul>

      <h3>One build or several</h3>
      <p>Keep one build unless two teams truly sell differently. One build means one set of facts to maintain, and the skills already serve every role.</p>
      <p>Separate builds drift apart, and each is another plugin to update. If two segments run different stages or messaging, split by segment, not by job title. Enterprise admins can then set which groups get which plugin.</p>
    

      <h2 id="seats">Admin, operator, seller: who changes what</h2>
      <p class="lede">Roles decide which skills and facts matter. Seats decide who is allowed to change them. Three people shape a configuration, and each controls a different part of it.</p>
      <p>On a plan of your own you hold all three seats, and nothing here conflicts. On a Team or Enterprise plan the seats are usually different people, so read yours first.</p>

      <div class="seats">
        <article class="seat">
          <header><h3>The admin</h3><p class="pointer">Decide what each tool may do.</p><p>An Owner or Primary Owner of the Claude organization. On Enterprise, also anyone whose custom role manages the organization's libraries. The role labeled Admin in the members list does not open the plugin settings.</p></header>
          <div class="quad">
            <div><h4>You control</h4><ul>
              <li>Which connectors the organization has. Each member still signs in with their own account.</li>
              <li>Tool permissions for everyone: always allow, needs approval or blocked. Members cannot loosen them.</li>
              <li>Each plugin's availability: not available, available to install, installed by default or required. On Enterprise, by group.</li>
              <li>What members may do themselves: create or upload plugins, share them, and publish them with or without review.</li>
            </ul></div>
            <div><h4>You do not write</h4><ul>
              <li>The sales facts. They belong to the operator.</li>
              <li>Your settings decide who gets the build and what it may do.</li>
            </ul></div>
            <div><h4>Your first move</h4><ol class="wk">
              <li><span>Add the connectors the plugin uses in organization settings. Making a plugin available does not add its connectors.</span></li>
              <li><span>Allow reads, put sends, posts and CRM writes on approval, and block what must never run.</span></li>
              <li><span>Decide the publishing policy before the operator submits anything.</span></li>
              <li><span>When the configured build arrives, set its availability, and decide whether the stock Sales plugin stays visible beside it.</span></li>
            </ol></div>
            <div><h4>Watch for</h4><ul>
              <li>"Installed by default" can be turned off by a member. "Required" cannot.</li>
              <li>A group's setting replaces the organization's for that group, and the most permissive setting wins across several groups.</li>
              <li>Items shared by someone who has left keep working until you stop the sharing.</li>
              <li>In Claude Code, a same-named plugin someone installed themselves loads in place of the copy synced from the organization.</li>
            </ul></div>
          </div>
        </article>

        <article class="seat">
          <header><h3>The operator</h3><p class="pointer">Write the company facts down once.</p><p>Whoever owns the sales process and its tools: revenue operations, sales operations, enablement, or a sales leader or founder doing that job.</p></header>
          <div class="quad">
            <div><h4>You control</h4><ul>
              <li>The company facts and skill behavior. This is the build.</li>
              <li>The reference page and the list of open decisions.</li>
              <li>What goes to review, and when.</li>
              <li>The update each time a source document changes.</li>
            </ul></div>
            <div><h4>You depend on</h4><ul>
              <li>The admin, for connectors, tool permissions and who receives the plugin.</li>
              <li>Sellers, for how deals are really worked.</li>
              <li>A reviewer's approval for each version, where publishing needs review.</li>
            </ul></div>
            <div><h4>Your first move</h4><ol class="wk">
              <li><span>Gather the source documents and name the open decisions.</span></li>
              <li><span>Customize a copy and review it like a contract.</span></li>
              <li><span>Try it with a few sellers by sharing it with them directly.</span></li>
              <li><span>Publish it to the organization, and agree with the admin who gets it.</span></li>
            </ol></div>
            <div><h4>Watch for</h4><ul>
              <li>Sellers' private copies and instructions drifting from yours. Collect the good parts, then ask them to retire the copies.</li>
              <li>Wording rules that clash with personal voice. Say in the build that company rules win.</li>
              <li>An update that never arrives. A published plugin changes only when you publish it again.</li>
            </ul></div>
          </div>
        </article>

        <article class="seat">
          <header><h3>The seller</h3><p class="pointer">Use the company build, and add your voice.</p><p>An individual contributor using Claude on the company's plan.</p></header>
          <div class="quad">
            <div><h4>You control</h4><ul>
              <li>Your sign-in to each tool. Claude acts with your access, so it sees the CRM records you can see.</li>
              <li>Your voice, signature and role, which <a class="skl" href="#skill-setup"><code>setup</code></a> learns.</li>
              <li>Your own project instructions and your scheduled routines.</li>
              <li>Your own plugins, where the organization lets members add them.</li>
            </ul></div>
            <div><h4>You cannot change</h4><ul>
              <li>A plugin the organization manages, or one a colleague shared with you.</li>
              <li>A plugin marked required. It stays on.</li>
              <li>Tool permissions set for the organization.</li>
              <li>Which connectors the organization has. You can ask for one where requests are turned on.</li>
            </ul></div>
            <div><h4>Your first move</h4><ol class="wk">
              <li><span>Open Plugins and look for a Sales plugin your organization already provides.</span></li>
              <li><span>If there is one, use it. Add only your voice, role and schedules.</span></li>
              <li><span>If there is none, start with the stock plugin and put your own facts in project instructions.</span></li>
              <li><span>Send what works, and every question a skill still asks, to whoever owns the sales process.</span></li>
            </ol></div>
            <div><h4>Watch for</h4><ul>
              <li>Two Sales plugins turned on. Keep one.</li>
              <li>Company facts in your instructions that disagree with the company build. Delete yours when the build arrives.</li>
              <li>Your own voice in anything you share.</li>
            </ul></div>
          </div>
        </article>
      </div>

      <h3>Where the seats overlap</h3>
      <p>Most conflicts come from the same thing being set in two places. Each row says what happens then, and how to keep it clean.</p>
      <div class="scroll">
        <table class="stack wide t-overlap">
          <thead><tr><th scope="col">What</th><th scope="col">Who can set it</th><th scope="col">If both do</th><th scope="col">Keep it clean by</th></tr></thead>
          <tbody>
            <tr><td data-l="What">Company facts</td><td data-l="Who can set it">A seller in project instructions. The operator in the plugin.</td><td data-l="If both do">A skill can see two answers. No documented rule says which wins.</td><td data-l="Keep it clean by">Keeping only personal facts in a seller's instructions once the company build exists</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="What">The plugin itself</td><td data-l="Who can set it">A seller's own customized copy. The organization's managed build.</td><td data-l="If both do">Members cannot edit the managed build. A private copy is a separate plugin with the same name.</td><td data-l="Keep it clean by">One Sales plugin per person. Offer a good private copy to the operator, then retire it</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="What">What a tool may do</td><td data-l="Who can set it">The admin, for everyone.</td><td data-l="If both do">The organization's setting wins. If a write is blocked, the skill keeps working and hands over a checklist to apply by hand.</td><td data-l="Keep it clean by">Putting hard limits in permissions, and telling sellers what is blocked</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="What">What data a skill sees</td><td data-l="Who can set it">The admin enables the connector. Each person signs in.</td><td data-l="If both do">Claude acts with each person's own access, so the same skill returns different records for different people.</td><td data-l="Keep it clean by">Fixing access in the CRM, not in the plugin</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="What">Wording</td><td data-l="Who can set it">The operator's wording rules. Each seller's learned voice.</td><td data-l="If both do">A draft has to satisfy both.</td><td data-l="Keep it clean by">Saying in the build that company wording rules outrank personal habit</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="What">Scheduled routines</td><td data-l="Who can set it">Each seller, for themselves.</td><td data-l="If both do">Every run acts within the organization's permissions.</td><td data-l="Keep it clean by">Keeping sends, posts and writes on approval for scheduled runs</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="What">New versions</td><td data-l="Who can set it">The operator publishes. A reviewer approves, where review is on.</td><td data-l="If both do">Members stay on the approved version until the next one is approved, and pick it up at their next session.</td><td data-l="Keep it clean by">Batching changes, and telling sellers what changed</td></tr>
          </tbody>
        </table>
      </div>
      <p class="note">The rules in this section come from Anthropic's help pages and docs, listed under Sources. Where they are silent, the table says so.</p>
    

      <h2 id="layers">Six things to configure, at three levels</h2>
      <p class="lede">Customizing the plugin does its work at the company level. The tool and person levels are settings around it.</p>

      <div class="lvls">
        <div class="lv">
          <div class="lv-head"><h3>Your tools</h3><span class="note">Set in connector settings.</span></div>
          <div class="tiles">
            <div class="tile"><h4>Connectors</h4><p>Which tools Claude can read and act in.</p><dl><dt>Set in</dt><dd>The plugin's connector list, plus each person's sign-in. Gmail, Google Calendar and Google Drive are connected in Claude's connector settings</dd><dt>Owner</dt><dd>An admin, or you on an individual plan</dd></dl></div>
            <div class="tile"><h4>Tool permissions</h4><p>Whether each action is always allowed, needs approval or is blocked.</p><dl><dt>Set in</dt><dd>Each connector's tool permissions</dd><dt>Owner</dt><dd>Organization owners on Team and Enterprise plans</dd></dl></div>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lv hot">
          <div class="lv-head"><h3>Your company</h3><span class="hl">the part you customize</span></div>
          <div class="tiles">
            <div class="tile"><h4>Company facts</h4><p>Your ICP, stages, qualification method, messaging, competitors and CRM habits.</p><dl><dt>Set in</dt><dd>Written into the skills</dd><dt>Owner</dt><dd>A sales leader, revenue operations or the founder</dd></dl></div>
            <div class="tile"><h4>Skill behavior</h4><p>How a skill works your way: which recorder it reads, how it logs, what it must never do.</p><dl><dt>Set in</dt><dd>Edits to individual skills</dd><dt>Owner</dt><dd>The same owner</dd></dl></div>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lv">
          <div class="lv-head"><h3>Each person</h3><span class="note">Learned or set by each seller.</span></div>
          <div class="tiles">
            <div class="tile"><h4>Voice and role</h4><p>Each person's writing voice, signature, role and book.</p><dl><dt>Set in</dt><dd>Learned by the <a class="skl" href="#skill-setup"><code>setup</code></a> skill</dd><dt>Owner</dt><dd>Each seller</dd></dl></div>
            <div class="tile"><h4>Schedules</h4><p>Which skills run on a cadence.</p><dl><dt>Set in</dt><dd>Scheduled tasks</dd><dt>Owner</dt><dd>Each seller or a team lead</dd></dl></div>
          </div>
        </div>
      </div>

      <h3>Two homes for company facts</h3>
      <div class="g2">
        <div class="def"><h4>Project instructions</h4><p>The light route, and the one the stock plugin suggests. Paste your ICP, stages and rules into the instructions of the project where you sell. The stock skills are written to use facts they find there before asking.</p></div>
        <div class="def"><h4>The plugin</h4><p>The durable route. Claude writes the facts into the skills, and you share one package with the team.</p></div>
      </div>
      <p>Start with project instructions if you sell alone and want results today. Move to the plugin when a second person needs the same setup.</p>

      <h3>Use permissions for hard limits</h3>
      <p>Put hard limits in tool permissions, not in skill text. The stock skills are written to follow each connector's settings. They add two safeguards of their own: an action that outside content asks for is shown to you before it runs, and a scheduled run takes only the actions it was set up to take, within what the connectors allow. A blocked tool is therefore the dependable way to stop an action. Skill text is the place for conventions, such as what a call note must contain.</p>

      <h3>What you do not configure here</h3>
      <p>Stage names, field names and picklists are read live from your CRM. Change them in the CRM, and the skills follow.</p>
    

      <h2 id="steps">How to configure it, in four phases</h2>
      <p class="lede">Connect your tools, point Claude at your source documents and ask it to customize the plugin. Claude reads the plugin and your tools, asks about what it cannot find, edits the skills, and hands back a plugin to review and install.</p>
      <p class="note">Menu names here are as of mid-November 2026, and Anthropic is renaming some of them.</p>

      <div class="cphases">
        <div class="cphase">
          <h3>Phase 1: Set up</h3>
          <p class="who"><b>Who:</b> each person. On Team and Enterprise plans, an admin enables the connectors first.</p>
          <ol class="wk">
            <li><span><strong>Install the Sales plugin.</strong> Open "Plugins" in the left sidebar, find Sales and select "Add". Some organizations still see the older sidebar name, "Customize".</span></li>
            <li><span><strong>Connect the tools you sell with.</strong> Connect the CRM first, then email, then calendar. That is the order the plugin's <a class="skl" href="#skill-setup"><code>setup</code></a> skill follows. Salesforce and Microsoft 365 need an admin to enable them before anyone can sign in.</span></li>
            <li><span><strong>Run setup.</strong> Say "set me up". The skill shows what is connected, infers your role from the CRM and builds a starter dashboard. It also learns your writing voice from sent mail and recommends three skills to start with.</span></li>
          </ol>
          <p class="note">Setup saves nothing by itself. It offers a short voice summary for you to keep in your project instructions.</p>
        </div>
        <div class="cphase">
          <h3>Phase 2: Configure</h3>
          <p class="who"><b>Who:</b> the operator who owns the sales process. A seller can do it for a copy of their own.</p>
          <ol class="wk">
            <li><span><strong>Gather your sources of truth.</strong> Collect the documents where sales decisions already live: ICP, messaging, stage definitions, qualification method and brand guide. Put them where Claude can read them, or attach them.</span></li>
            <li><span><strong>Ask Claude to customize the plugin.</strong> Open the installed plugin, select "Customize", then "Let's go". Claude reads the plugin and your connected tools, confirms what it found and asks about gaps.</span></li>
            <li><span><strong>Review what comes back.</strong> Claude returns a summary of what it learned and a plugin card. Open the skills you will use every day and read them. Then select "Save plugin".</span></li>
          </ol>
        </div>
        <div class="cphase">
          <h3>Phase 3: Control</h3>
          <p class="who"><b>Who:</b> the admin for permissions. Each person for their own schedules.</p>
          <ol class="wk">
            <li><span><strong>Set tool permissions.</strong> Each connector tool can be set to "Always allow", "Needs approval" or "Blocked". A sound start is reads allowed, with sends, posts and CRM writes on approval. On Team and Enterprise plans, owners set this for the whole organization.</span></li>
            <li><span><strong>Schedule the routines.</strong> The <a class="skl" href="#skill-setup"><code>setup</code></a> skill offers four: a daily briefing, a CRM hygiene check, an end-of-day close-out and a weekly wrap. Create them from "Scheduled", then "New task".</span></li>
            <li><span><strong>Decide what a scheduled run may do.</strong> The plugin's README advises keeping send, post and write tools on approval for scheduled runs. By design, a scheduled run takes only the actions you set it up to take. Anything else it finds becomes a proposal in its output.</span></li>
          </ol>
        </div>
        <div class="cphase">
          <h3>Phase 4: Share and maintain</h3>
          <p class="who"><b>Who:</b> the operator publishes. The admin sets who receives it.</p>
          <ol class="wk">
            <li><span><strong>Roll it out.</strong> Anthropic's tutorial lists three routes: send the plugin file, host it in a Git repository set up as a marketplace, or have an admin add it for the organization.</span></li>
            <li><span><strong>Choose how people get it.</strong> Organization owners set each plugin as available to install, installed by default, required or not available. Enterprise admins can vary that by group.</span></li>
            <li><span><strong>Keep it current.</strong> When a source document changes, tell Claude what changed and save the plugin again. A plugin shared directly updates for its recipients. One published to the organization must be published again.</span></li>
          </ol>
        </div>
      </div>

      <h3>A prompt to start from</h3>
      <div class="codeblk">
        <button class="btn sm" type="button" id="copy-prompt" data-copy="prompt-text">Copy</button>
<pre id="prompt-text">Customize the Sales plugin for my company.
Our sources of truth are the documents in [folder or links].
Use only what those documents and our connected tools say.
Where something is undecided, list it as an open question. Do not fill it in.
Do not copy prices, customer names or email addresses into the plugin.
Do not rename the plugin or any skill.
When you finish, show what changed in each skill and what you could not find.</pre>
      </div>
      <p>You do not need to cover everything in one session. Come back, tell Claude what to add and save again.</p>
      <p>If your organization already distributes the Sales plugin, members cannot edit that managed copy. Its owner should run the customization.</p>

      <h3>In Claude Code</h3>
      <p>The plugin's README gives two commands for Claude Code. Plugins saved to your Claude account also sync there when you sign in with that account.</p>
      <p>Do one or the other. A copy you install from the marketplace loads in place of a same-named plugin synced from your account, so the stock plugin would stand in for your company's configured build.</p>
      <div class="codeblk">
        <button class="btn sm" type="button" id="copy-cli" data-copy="cli-text">Copy</button>
<pre id="cli-text">claude plugin marketplace add anthropics/knowledge-work-plugins
claude plugin install sales@knowledge-work-plugins</pre>
      </div>

      <div class="box quick">
        <h3>Short on time?</h3>
        <ol class="wk">
          <li><span>Connect the CRM and email, then run setup.</span></li>
          <li><span>Paste your ICP and stage definitions into your project instructions.</span></li>
          <li><span>Customize the plugin when you want the setup shared.</span></li>
        </ol>
      </div>

      <h3>Three rules for what goes in</h3>
      <ul>
        <li><strong>Write decisions, not aspirations.</strong> If the team does not follow a stage definition, the plugin will enforce a fiction.</li>
        <li><strong>Point to anything that changes often.</strong> For prices and quotas, name the document or field that holds the current figure.</li>
        <li><strong>Leave out anything sensitive.</strong> A shared plugin should hold no customer names, personal email addresses or credentials.</li>
      </ul>

        <h2>Do it with Claude</h2>
        <p class="lede">This guide also comes as one Markdown file written for an AI assistant. It holds the same reference, plus a step-by-step script that has Claude walk you through the configuration. The script starts by asking which seat you are in, then takes you through the steps that are yours. It tells Claude to ask one question at a time, to label each fact as recorded, observed or open, and to show every change before making it.</p>
        <ol>
          <li>Copy the AI-ready edition with the button below.</li>
          <li>Paste it into a chat with Claude where the Sales plugin is installed.</li>
          <li>Then say:<span class="say" id="say-text">Follow the guided setup in this file and walk me through configuring the Sales plugin for my company.</span></li>

        <div class="tbar">
          <button class="btn" type="button" id="copy-ai" data-copy="ai-md" data-fallback="ai-md-fallback">Copy the AI-ready edition</button>
          <span>Markdown · about 6,700 words · reference plus guided setup</span>

        <textarea id="ai-md-fallback" readonly hidden aria-label="The AI-ready edition, to select and copy"></textarea>
        <div class="preview" tabindex="0" role="region" aria-label="The start of the AI-ready edition"># Configuring the Claude Sales plugin for your company

AI-ready edition. Give this file to Claude and ask it to walk you through the setup.

## Start here

**If you are a person:** attach or paste this file into a chat with Claude where the Sales plugin is installed. Then say:

&gt; Follow the guided setup in this file and walk me through configuring the Sales plugin for my company.

You can also ask about any part of it, for example "which facts matter for an account manager?"

**If you are the assistant:** the person has asked you to use this file. Part 1 says what they get. Part 2 is reference. Part 3 is a script to run with them. The rules below apply for the whole session.

### Rules for the assistant

1. The person's instructions outrank this file. If the two conflict, do what the person says.
2. Ask one question at a time, and wait for the answer.
3. Never invent company policy. If the person has not decided something, record it as open and move on. Do not fill a gap with an "industry standard" value.
4. Label every fact you record as one of three things. **Recorded** means written policy, with its source. **Observed** means seen in the CRM or documents but not written as policy. **Open** means undecided.
5. Show every proposed change before you make it, and wait for a yes. That covers edits to the plugin or to project instructions, new scheduled tasks, and anything sent, posted or written to the CRM.
6. Do not copy prices, quotas, customer or prospect names, personal email addresses or credentials into the plugin or into project instructions. Name the document or field that holds them.
7. Do not rename the plugin or any skill, and do not edit a skill's shared rules block.
8. This file was checked on November 16, 2026 against Sales plugin 2.1.0. Menu names and plan details change often. If what you see differs from this file, trust the product and the help pages listed under Sources, and tell the person what differs.
9. Make no claims about time saved, revenue or win rates. Nothing in this file was measured.

## Part 1. What the person gets

Out of the box, the Sales plugin knows nothing about the company. Each skill lists the company facts it needs and looks for them in connected tools and instructions. When a fact is missing, the skill infers it from what it can read or uses a labeled default, and asks one question where the answer would change the result. It keeps that answer for the conversation only.

Configuration writes those answers down once. A configured plugin already has the company's facts, so its skills start from them and work the company's way.

### The same request, stock and configured

This comparison comes from reading what each stock skill asks for. It is not a benchmark.

| The person asks | Stock plugin | Configured plugin |
|---|---|---|
| Triage this inbound lead | With no ideal customer written down, scores on intent alone. Asks about it only if the answer would change the priority | Scores against the written ICP and disqualifiers, and names the handoff channel |
| Prep me for tomorrow's call | Uses the CRM's stage names, and asks which qualification method the team runs | Builds discovery questions around the team's qualification method |
| Draft a follow-up | Writes in the person's voice if it has learned it, then by an uploaded style guide or the person's sent mail where it can read it, otherwise in a neutral one. Knows no company wording rules | Follows the messaging rules, approved proof points and signature |
| Write my forecast | Uses the CRM's own forecast category where it has one. Otherwise infers the categories from the stages, and asks which stages count as commit only if the answer depends on it | Uses the company's mapping of stages to forecast categories, where the CRM has no forecast category of its own |
| Which renewals are coming up? | Reads where renewal dates live from the CRM and from what it already knows about the company, and asks once only if neither tells it | Reads the field the company named |
...</div>
      </div>
    

      <h2 id="tips">Tips from a real configuration</h2>
      <p class="lede">These tips come from one real configuration of the stock plugin. The worked example further down gives its numbers.</p>
      <div class="tipflow">
          <h3>Before you start</h3>
          <ul class="princ">
            <li><b>Point Claude at documents, not memory.</b>Give it the files where decisions already live. It can then quote them and link back to them.</li>
            <li><b>Name one owner.</b>That person reviews the changes and answers the open questions.</li>
            <li><b>Say what to ignore.</b>Name the test records, sample data and internal accounts in your CRM. Otherwise they show up in pipeline reviews.</li>
          </ul>
          <h3>While configuring</h3>
          <ul class="princ">
            <li><b>Give each skill only the facts it uses.</b>In the real build, every skill got a short company section assembled from shared blocks. A call-prep skill does not need routing rules.</li>
            <li><b>Keep one reference page inside the plugin.</b>It holds every fact, a link to each source document and the list of open decisions.</li>
            <li><b>Label how sure each fact is.</b>The real build marked facts as recorded, observed or open. Recorded means written policy, observed means seen in the data, and open means undecided.</li>
            <li><b>Never let the configuration invent policy.</b>The company had no agreed stage exit criteria. The build kept a proposed mapping on the open list, switched off until the owner approves it.</li>
            <li><b>Encode your wording rules.</b>List banned words, required phrases and claims you cannot make. They then apply to every draft.</li>
            <li><b>Separate what exists from what is planned.</b>List shipped capabilities apart from the roadmap, so no draft sells the roadmap as built.</li>
            <li><b>Replace vendor-specific passages.</b>A few stock passages describe one vendor's mechanics, such as a CRM audit query. Swap in your own tool's equivalent.</li>
            <li><b>Trim the connector list.</b>The stock plugin lists 23 tool servers. The real build kept four and added the meeting recorder in use.</li>
            <li><b>Keep every name.</b>Do not rename the plugin or its skills. People, saved prompts and schedules call them by name.</li>
          </ul>
          <h3>After configuring</h3>
          <ul class="princ">
            <li><b>Watch skill length.</b>Every added paragraph is read on every run. The real build grew the skills' total word count by about 60 percent, and seven skills passed 3,000 words. Claude's own plugin-authoring guidance suggests staying under about 3,000 words per skill.</li>
            <li><b>Check it like a contract.</b>Ask for a before-and-after of each skill. Search the finished plugin for every banned word. Confirm skill names and the shared rules are unchanged.</li>
            <li><b>Ask what is still missing.</b>End by asking which company facts Claude could not find. That list is your next session.</li>
            <li><b>Start with drafts.</b>Keep sends, posts and CRM writes on approval until you trust the output.</li>
            <li><b>Do not overread small samples.</b>The <a class="skl" href="#skill-setup"><code>setup</code></a> skill will not guess a voice from fewer than five sent emails. Let company wording rules outrank a learned personal habit.</li>
          </ul>
      </div>
    

      <h2 id="pitfalls">Pitfalls to avoid</h2>
      <p class="lede">Most configuration mistakes come from writing down too much, or writing down what nobody decided.</p>
      <div class="scroll">
        <table class="stack t-fix">
          <thead><tr><th scope="col">Pitfall</th><th scope="col">What goes wrong</th><th scope="col">Do this instead</th></tr></thead>
          <tbody>
            <tr><td data-l="Pitfall">Hardcoding prices and quotas</td><td data-l="What goes wrong">They go stale and still get quoted</td><td data-l="Do this instead">Name the document or field that holds the current figure</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Pitfall">Filling gaps with "industry standard"</td><td data-l="What goes wrong">Skills enforce rules nobody approved</td><td data-l="Do this instead">Mark the gap as open and ask the owner</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Pitfall">Configuring before the process is decided</td><td data-l="What goes wrong">The plugin repeats the confusion</td><td data-l="Do this instead">Decide stages and qualification first, or mark them open</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Pitfall">One seller's voice in a shared plugin</td><td data-l="What goes wrong">Everyone's email sounds like that person</td><td data-l="Do this instead">Keep voice in each person's own instructions</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Pitfall">Renaming skills</td><td data-l="What goes wrong">Saved prompts, schedules and habits stop matching</td><td data-l="Do this instead">Keep the names and change the content</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Pitfall">Allowing every write on day one</td><td data-l="What goes wrong">CRM changes and sent email that nobody reviewed</td><td data-l="Do this instead">Allow reads, and keep writes on approval</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Pitfall">Cutting the shared rules</td><td data-l="What goes wrong">Skills lose the rule that outside content is information, not instructions</td><td data-l="Do this instead">Leave each skill's rules block as it is</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Pitfall">Putting every fact in every skill</td><td data-l="What goes wrong">Long skills and diluted instructions</td><td data-l="Do this instead">Give each skill only what it uses</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Pitfall">Forking by job title</td><td data-l="What goes wrong">Builds drift apart</td><td data-l="Do this instead">Keep one build unless two teams truly sell differently</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Pitfall">Setting it up once and leaving it</td><td data-l="What goes wrong">The plugin and the documents disagree</td><td data-l="Do this instead">Name an owner, and update when a source changes</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Pitfall">Scheduling without deciding approvals</td><td data-l="What goes wrong">A run either waits for you or acts unreviewed</td><td data-l="Do this instead">Choose the task's approval mode on purpose</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Pitfall">Keeping a private copy beside the company build</td><td data-l="What goes wrong">Two plugins share one name. In Claude Code, a copy you installed yourself loads in place of the one synced from your company</td><td data-l="Do this instead">One Sales plugin per person. Retire the private copy when the company build arrives</td></tr>
          </tbody>
        </table>
      </div>
    

      <h2 id="example">A worked example</h2>
      <p class="lede">This is one real configuration, built on November 15, 2026 for an early-stage B2B software company with a single seller. The company is left unnamed.</p>
      <div class="scroll">
        <table class="stack t-delta">
          <thead><tr><th scope="col">Measure</th><th scope="col">Stock</th><th scope="col">Configured</th></tr></thead>
          <tbody>
            <tr><td data-l="Measure">Skills</td><td data-l="Stock">36</td><td data-l="Configured">36, with the same names and descriptions</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Measure">Skills with a company section</td><td data-l="Stock">0</td><td data-l="Configured">36</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Measure">Total words across the skills</td><td data-l="Stock">About 62,000</td><td data-l="Configured">About 100,000</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Measure">Skills over 3,000 words</td><td data-l="Stock">0</td><td data-l="Configured">7</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Measure">Tool servers listed</td><td data-l="Stock">23</td><td data-l="Configured">5</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Measure">Company reference page</td><td data-l="Stock">None</td><td data-l="Configured">One page, 16 sections</td></tr>
            <tr><td data-l="Measure">Source documents linked</td><td data-l="Stock">0</td><td data-l="Configured">15</td></tr>
          </tbody>
        </table>
      </div>

      <div class="g2">
        <div class="def"><h3>What Claude found</h3><p>One CRM on its default pipeline, work email and calendar, a shared document drive and a meeting recorder. No team chat, enrichment tool or sales-engagement tool. The sales decisions lived in 15 documents, including a messaging framework, ICP and personas, the offer and its terms, and a brand guide.</p></div>
        <div class="def"><h3>What changed</h3><p>Every skill gained a company section, built from 12 shared blocks plus notes written for that skill. Each skill's tool table now names the tools in use, and its opening step reads company facts instead of asking for them. Six skills had whole passages rewritten for those tools. Five more had smaller edits.</p></div>
        <div class="def"><h3>What stayed out</h3><p>No prices, prospect names or email addresses were copied into the plugin. No stage exit criteria were invented: a proposed mapping waits on the owner's approval. Skill names, descriptions and the shared rules block are identical to stock.</p></div>
        <div class="def"><h3>How it was checked</h3><p>Every skill was compared with its original, line by line. The finished package was searched for banned words. Names, descriptions and rules were confirmed unchanged.</p></div>
      </div>

      <h3>What is still open</h3>
      <div class="box">
        <p>Four decisions went back to the owner: the proposed stage mapping, what amount a deal should carry, where prospect documents are saved and whether to finish CRM onboarding.</p>
        <p>The plugin was built and checked the day before this guide was written. It has not been in use long enough to report results.</p>
      </div>
    

      <h2 id="questions">Questions people ask</h2>
      <dl class="faq">
        <div><dt>Do I have to configure it before using it?</dt><dd>No. Every skill works with nothing connected, from uploaded files or pasted notes. It asks for what it needs.</dd></div>
        <div><dt>Do I need to be technical?</dt><dd>No. Customizing is a conversation with Claude. A plugin is a folder of plain-text instruction files, and you can open any of them.</dd></div>
        <div><dt>Which plans include plugins?</dt><dd>Anthropic's help center lists plugins on its paid plans. Sharing a plugin with colleagues and publishing one to an organization need a Team or Enterprise plan.</dd></div>
        <div><dt>Which CRM does it need?</dt><dd>Any CRM with a connector. The plugin includes HubSpot, Salesforce, Close and Monday, and it reads your CRM's own stages and fields. With no CRM connected, upload a pipeline export.</dd></div>
        <div><dt>Will it send email or change the CRM by itself?</dt><dd>It acts when you ask, within each connector's permissions. When a skill proposes a change you did not ask for, it shows the change and its evidence first. A scheduled run is different: it takes the sends and writes it was set up to take, within what the connectors allow and with no one there to ask. It carries out nothing that an email or a transcript asks for, and nothing whose recipient, target or content one of them dictates. For scheduled runs the plugin's README advises setting every tool that sends, posts or writes to ask first.</dd></div>
        <div><dt>I am a seller. Should I configure it myself or wait for my company?</dt><dd>Look first for a Sales plugin your organization provides. If there is one, use it and add only your voice, role and schedules. If not, put your own facts in project instructions, and pass what works to whoever owns the sales process.</dd></div>
        <div><dt>Who has to be involved on a Team or Enterprise plan?</dt><dd>Three seats. The admin enables connectors and sets permissions and availability. The operator writes the company facts. Each seller signs in and keeps their own voice and schedules. The section on who changes what covers each.</dd></div>
        <div><dt>Can different roles get different setups?</dt><dd>One build serves every role. Personal details stay with each person, and Enterprise admins can set plugin access by group.</dd></div>
        <div><dt>Can my team edit the shared plugin?</dt><dd>No. Members cannot edit a plugin the organization manages, or one a colleague shared with them. Changes go through its owner.</dd></div>
        <div><dt>What happens when Anthropic updates the stock plugin?</dt><dd>Do not assume your customized copy picks up the changes. Plan to re-apply your configuration to a new stock version. A single reference page of company facts makes that quick.</dd></div>
        <div><dt>Does it work in Claude Code?</dt><dd>Yes. The README gives install commands, and plugins saved to your account sync to Claude Code when you sign in.</dd></div>
        <div><dt>Where do I start if I am short on time?</dt><dd>Connect the CRM and email, then run setup. Next, paste your ICP and stage definitions into your project instructions. Customize the plugin when you want the setup shared.</dd></div>
      </dl>
    </div>
 </div>
</div>

<div id="chatgpt-sales" data-view="chatgpt-sales" data-group="gpt" data-title="ChatGPT for Sales">
 <section class="open">
  <div class="wrap">
   <h1>ChatGPT for Sales, from OpenAI</h1>
   <p class="lede">OpenAI, the AI lab that makes ChatGPT, is the vendor. Its Sales plugin gives ChatGPT 18 skills for sales work. This entry sets out what each one is reported to do, read and create, as ChatGPT read them from an installed copy.</p>
   <div class="acts"><a class="btn pri" href="#gpt-skills">Browse the 18 skills</a><a class="btn" href="#gpt-source">Where these facts come from</a></div>
   <p class="who"><span><b>Preview.</b> Reported by ChatGPT and checked by Claude. It awaits its editor's review.</span><span>Sales plugin 1.1.7, read on November 22, 2026</span><span>For revenue leaders and operators</span></p>
  </div>
 </section>

 <section class="sec">
  <div class="wrap">
   <h2 id="gpt-source">Where these facts come from</h2>
   <p class="lead2">The plugin's files are not public, so this entry rests on a report and says so. Read it as one relay of what the plugin's own text says it does, not as a test of what it does.</p>
   <div class="g3 mt">
    <div class="def"><h3>Who read the plugin</h3><p>ChatGPT, an OpenAI model, in the editor's own account with the plugin installed. On November 22, 2026 it was given <a href="kit/chatgpt/inventory-prompt.md">a fixed prompt</a> that asked for an inventory of the plugin's own text and nothing else. It reported version 1.1.7, 18 skills and the full instructions of each. It ran no skill and called no connected app.</p></div>
    <div class="def"><h3>How the report was checked</h3><p>By Claude, an Anthropic model. The prompt held back two facts from OpenAI's public page as a test: the count of skills and eight of their names. The report matched both. Every cross-reference between skills resolves to one of the 18, and what it says of publisher, capabilities and how to call the plugin agrees with OpenAI's pages, opened again on November 22, 2026.</p></div>
    <div class="def"><h3>What that does not show</h3><p>We did not read the files, so a detail may be wrong or missing. No workflow was run, so nothing here says how well one works. The wording is ours: the plugin is quoted once, for its first prompt. Our own additions are marked: the grouping, who a skill is for, its GTM job and each example request.</p></div>
   </div>
  </div>
 </section>

 <section class="sec soft">
  <div class="wrap">
   <h2 id="gpt-glance">At a glance</h2>
   <dl class="glance"><div><dt>Provider and offering</dt><dd><p>OpenAI, a vendor. <a href="https://openai.com/index/introducing-chatgpt-for-sales/">ChatGPT for Sales</a> was announced on November 17, 2026. The copy read for this page is version 1.1.7.</p></dd></div><div><dt>Category</dt><dd><p>AI lab</p></dd></div><div><dt>In one sentence</dt><dd><p>A Sales plugin for ChatGPT with 18 skills, one that routes each request and 17 workflows, for researching accounts, preparing and following up meetings, planning deals, making the case to a buyer, reviewing a forecast and coaching reps.</p></dd></div><div><dt>Go-to-market (GTM) jobs it covers</dt><dd><ul class="jobs"><li id="gjob-lead-qualification"><span>Lead qualification</span><span class="chips"><a class="chip" It does not record which are connected. <a href="#gpt-apps">The list</a>.</p></dd></div><div><dt>Who can get it</dt><dd><p>OpenAI's <a href="https://help.openai.com/en/articles/20001707-getting-started-with-chatgpt-for-sales">help page</a> says the Sales plugin is on every ChatGPT plan, the free one included. Partner plugins depend on plan, region and workspace settings, and installing Sales connects no partner account. The plugin's page says to use it in ChatGPT Work or in Codex. No price is stated.</p></dd></div><div><dt>Controls</dt><dd><p>Each workflow carries its own rules for what needs the user's say-so, and no workflow is reported to send a message or change a CRM record on its first pass. Admins of Business and Enterprise workspaces decide whether the plugin and partner plugins are available or installed, and review each app's access and allowed actions. The help page points to OpenAI's pages on security and compliance and does not itself say how customer data is kept or used.</p></dd></div><div><dt>What the provider reports</dt><dd><p>No measured result. The announcement carries statements from customers. One has a number: a co-founder at a customer company estimates about 65% less manual effort for some activities when HubSpot, a vendor's product, is used with ChatGPT for Sales.</p></dd></div><div><dt>Evidence in the register</dt><dd><p>None yet.</p></dd></div><div><dt>Not public or not known</dt><dd><p>The plugin's files are not public, and we have not read them. OpenAI's <a href="https://github.com/openai/role-specific-plugins">role-specific-plugins</a> repository held only a placeholder note. Not known: whether each workflow behaves as its instructions say, since none was run. Also not known: which model runs a workflow, how any result is measured and the names behind 13 integrations that the installed copy stores as codes.</p></dd></div><div><dt>On this site</dt><dd><p>This entry. It rates nothing. Our read of fit, overlap and value, as the <a href="#overview">Claude Sales plugin entry</a> gives it, waits for the workflows to be run.</p></dd></div><div><dt>Last checked</dt><dd><p>November 22, 2026: the installed copy through ChatGPT, and OpenAI's plugin page, help page and announcement by Claude.</p></dd></div></dl>
  </div>
 </section>

 <section class="sec">
  <div class="wrap">
   <h2 id="gpt-skills">The 18 skills</h2>
   <p class="lead2">A user calls the plugin with @Sales and says what they want. One skill, <a class="chip" href="#gpt-index">index</a>, reads the request and hands it to the workflow that fits. The other 17 are reached through it.</p>
   <ul class="gtally">
    <li><b>7 of 18</b> describe a rerun on a schedule, and each asks before one is set up.</li>
    <li><b>5 of 18</b> can be run by a manager for a named seller.</li>
    <li><b>18 of 18</b> name a way to work from files or pasted text when no app is connected.</li>
    <li><b>None</b> is reported to send a message or change a CRM record on its first pass.</li>
   </ul>
   <p class="note">The plugin sets no order for its skills. They are grouped here by the work they do, which is our read.</p>
   <div class="ggrp"><h3 id="gpt-g-start">Start here <small>The skill that routes every request, and a fictional demo.</small></h3>
<details class="gsk" id="gpt-index">
<summary><span class="nm"><code>index</code></span><span class="tg">The router</span><span class="fitm"><span class="t">Anyone</span></span><span class="pm" aria-hidden="true"></span></summary>
<div class="body">
<p class="does">Picks the Sales workflow that fits a request, or introduces the plugin.</p>
<div class="gcard">
<div class="runhead"><h3>Use it</h3><p>As ChatGPT reported the skill's instructions. The example request is ours and was not run.</p></div>
<dl class="runkv">
<div><dt>When</dt><dd>Any request with a seller, a leader, a customer, a prospect, a partner, an account or an opportunity in it. A broad question about how to begin gets the introduction.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Ask</dt><dd><p class="sayline"><span class="sayt" id="gask-index">@Sales what can you do for a renewal that is due next month?</span><button class="btn sm jsonly" type="button" data-copy="gask-index">Copy</button></p></dd></div>
<div><dt>Give it</dt><dd>The request itself.</dd></div>
<div><dt>You get</dt><dd>A handoff to the chosen workflow. Asked how to begin, it gives a short tour with sample requests and offers the fictional demo. It lists every skill on request.</dd></div>
</dl>
</div>
<dl class="kv">
<div><dt>Reads</dt><dd>The descriptions of every skill, then the instructions of the one it chooses and the plugin&#x27;s shared rules.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Can create or change</dt><dd>Nothing.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Asks first</dt><dd>Not stated.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Will not</dt><dd>Reading the catalog does not stand in for reading the chosen workflow. A general document-making skill may not take over a request that a Sales workflow owns.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Whose work it covers</dt><dd>The whole plugin. Whether a manager may act for a named seller is left to the workflow it chooses.</dd></div>
<div><dt>On a schedule</dt><dd>Not stated.</dd></div>
<div><dt>With no app connected</dt><dd>It can introduce the plugin and route a request from its wording alone. The chosen workflow decides what evidence is needed.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Company context it uses</dt><dd>Not stated.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Before and after</dt><dd>All 17 other skills. A request for a stand-alone deck built from a complete set of customer facts goes to <a class="skl" href="#gpt-sales-presentations"><code>sales-presentations</code></a>.</dd></div>
<div><dt>GTM job <small>Our read</small></dt><dd>Outside the site's list of 13 jobs.</dd></div>
</dl>
<div class="skfoot jsonly"><button class="btn sm" type="button" disabled>Previous</button><button class="btn sm" type="button" data-gstep="demo-exec-and-seller-dash" data-of="index">Next</button><button class="btn sm shut" type="button" data-gshut="index">Close <code>index</code></button></div>

</details>
<details class="gsk" id="gpt-demo-exec-and-seller-dash">
<summary><span class="nm"><code>demo-exec-and-seller-dash</code></span><span class="tg">The fictional demo</span><span class="fitm"><span class="t">Anyone</span></span><span class="pm" aria-hidden="true"></span></summary>
<div class="body">
<p class="does">Walks through a made-up company: a division leader&#x27;s dashboard, a seller&#x27;s book, a customer deck that already exists and a proposed CRM update.</p>
<div class="gcard">
<div class="runhead"><h3>Use it</h3><p>As ChatGPT reported the skill's instructions. The example request is ours and was not run.</p></div>
<dl class="runkv">
<div><dt>When</dt><dd>Only when someone asks for the demo outright.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Ask</dt><dd><p class="sayline"><span class="sayt" id="gask-demo-exec-and-seller-dash">@Sales run the fictional demo</span><button class="btn sm jsonly" type="button" data-copy="gask-demo-exec-and-seller-dash">Copy</button></p></dd></div>
<div><dt>Give it</dt><dd>The request. Later replies choose the next step.</dd></div>
<div><dt>You get</dt><dd>A conversation that leads from step to step, with links to two ready-made dashboards, the choice of seeing a deck or a sample email, and a proposed CRM change shown field by field, four fields in all. At the end it offers up to three workflows to try on real data.</dd></div>
</dl>
</div>
<dl class="kv">
<div><dt>Reads</dt><dd>Bundled fictional material only: company background, leadership and seller data, meeting notes and rules for the CRM update. Local files imitate records from Salesforce, Gmail, Google Calendar, Granola, Slack and Google Drive, each a vendor&#x27;s product.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Can create or change</dt><dd>Nothing outside the chat in the hosted walk-through. The example email stays in the chat, unsent.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Asks first</dt><dd>A simulated save completes only after a separate request for exactly the proposal that was reviewed, and the walk-through then confirms that no real record changed.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Will not</dt><dd>It keeps real records out of the fiction and takes no real action: no search for connected providers, no installed app, no email, task, CRM change, meeting or published site. The ready-made deck is neither copied nor changed.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Whose work it covers</dt><dd>Fictional people and accounts only. A manager cannot put a real seller in their place.</dd></div>
<div><dt>On a schedule</dt><dd>It tells itself not to start a schedule.</dd></div>
<div><dt>With no app connected</dt><dd>Built to run with nothing connected.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Company context it uses</dt><dd>None that is real.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Before and after</dt><dd>Afterwards it offers a real seller dashboard, meeting preparation or call follow-up with proposed CRM updates.</dd></div>
<div><dt>GTM job <small>Our read</small></dt><dd>Outside the site's list of 13 jobs.</dd></div>
</dl>

<div class="ggrp"><h3 id="gpt-g-accounts">Accounts and prospecting <small>Finding companies and people, and understanding an account.</small></h3>
<details class="gsk" id="gpt-enrich-company-and-contact-data">
<summary><span class="nm"><code>enrich-company-and-contact-data</code></span><span class="tg">Find and enrich companies and contacts</span><span class="fitm"><span class="t">Sellers</span></span><span class="pm" aria-hidden="true"></span></summary>
<div class="body">
<p class="does">Completes the records you hand it, searches for companies or people who fit your criteria, or sorts a defined set into tiers and segments.</p>
<div class="gcard">
<div class="runhead"><h3>Use it</h3><p>As ChatGPT reported the skill's instructions. The example request is ours and was not run.</p></div>
<dl class="runkv">
<div><dt>When</dt><dd>Finding a buyer, identifying contacts, matching an ideal customer profile (ICP), completing missing fields, resolving duplicates, finding lookalikes or scanning a defined market signal. Not for meeting preparation, outreach to a known recipient or ranking a book you already own.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Ask</dt><dd><p class="sayline"><span class="sayt" id="gask-enrich-company-and-contact-data">@Sales find 20 companies in <span class="slot">[region]</span> that match our ICP and the head of operations at each</span><button class="btn sm jsonly" type="button" data-copy="gask-enrich-company-and-contact-data">Copy</button></p></dd></div>
<div><dt>Give it</dt><dd>A working set, or the criteria or seed companies to search from. Optional: a CSV or an export, domains, emails, the fields wanted, ICP rules, role, seniority, geography, size, technology, a ranking rule and a limit.</dd></div>
<div><dt>You get</dt><dd>A table of the completed records, a list of full and partial matches, or a table that compares, tiers or flags signals. Every row shows its evidence, how sure the match is and what stayed unresolved, with the sources and how much ground was covered.</dd></div>
</dl>
</div>
<dl class="kv">
<div><dt>Reads</dt><dd>Data providers for facts about companies and people, the technology they use, buying intent and similar companies. The CRM, to tell who is already known, owned or in a deal, and to catch duplicates. Your files for the ICP, target lists, territories and segment rules, and any rows you supply. Public sources only to confirm a point or fill a narrow gap.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Can create or change</dt><dd>Nothing at first. Afterwards it can export the table to a spreadsheet with its evidence and open questions intact. For the CRM it only proposes field changes: writing them is a separate action.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Asks first</dt><dd>Explicit approval before any search, export or enrichment that uses up provider credits, and before it touches personal contact data. A CRM update needs its own approval.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Will not</dt><dd>It does not invent contacts or fields, swap a data provider for broad web search or computer use, widen the scope quietly or claim to cover a whole market. It does not write to the CRM, create records or send outreach by itself.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Whose work it covers</dt><dd>A single company, a few contacts or a supplied list. It can also run one bounded search across a territory or a market.</dd></div>
<div><dt>On a schedule</dt><dd>Not stated.</dd></div>
<div><dt>With no app connected</dt><dd>It uses equivalent data you have already supplied and keeps to those companies and fields. If a provider is needed, it asks for the fewest fields that would let it check the filters.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Company context it uses</dt><dd>Your ICP and its hard limits, territories, segments, target lists, the conventions you follow when enriching records and any rule for ranking.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Before and after</dt><dd>Account prioritization or company research, as the next selling step.</dd></div>
<div><dt>GTM job <small>Our read</small></dt><dd>Lead qualification</dd></div>
</dl>
<div class="skfoot jsonly"><button class="btn sm" type="button" data-gstep="demo-exec-and-seller-dash" data-of="enrich-company-and-contact-data">Previous</button><button class="btn sm" type="button" data-gstep="sales-company-research" data-of="enrich-company-and-contact-data">Next</button><button class="btn sm shut" type="button" data-gshut="enrich-company-and-contact-data">Close <code>enrich-company-and-contact-data</code></button></div>

<details class="gsk" id="gpt-sales-company-research">
<summary><span class="nm"><code>sales-company-research</code></span><span class="tg">Research a company and shape the outreach</span><span class="fitm"><span class="t">Sellers</span></span><span class="pm" aria-hidden="true"></span></summary>
<div class="body">
<p class="does">Researches a company, partner or audience you already know, explains the fit and drafts an approach.</p>
<div class="gcard">
<div class="runhead"><h3>Use it</h3><p>As ChatGPT reported the skill's instructions. The example request is ours and was not run.</p></div>
<dl class="runkv">
<div><dt>When</dt><dd>Judging the fit of a company or a partner. Also mapping who could refer you, checking a reputation and finding an angle for people you already know you want to reach. Working out who to contact belongs to the enrichment skill.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Ask</dt><dd><p class="sayline"><span class="sayt" id="gask-sales-company-research">@Sales research <span class="slot">[company]</span> and draft an outreach approach for its finance lead</span><button class="btn sm jsonly" type="button" data-copy="gask-sales-company-research">Copy</button></p></dd></div>
<div><dt>Give it</dt><dd>Something to anchor the research (a company, a domain, a partner type, a product or an offer) and the commercial purpose. Optional: the audience, approved positioning, proof points, partner criteria, the channel, the kind of draft wanted and outreach guardrails.</dd></div>
<div><dt>You get</dt><dd>Sourced context, fit and motion, a recommended approach and a draft for the channel asked for, with fit confidence, risks and gaps. The draft is not sent.</dd></div>
</dl>
</div>
<dl class="kv">
<div><dt>Reads</dt><dd>Data providers for background on the company, its partners and people, and for signals. The CRM for who owns the account, where it stands and any earlier contact. Your files for positioning, proof and what you look for in a partner. Public research for facts the company controls, its reputation, partner programs and the market.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Can create or change</dt><dd>Nothing outside the chat. A later option is a short research brief or an internal note, once the content has been reviewed. Where that would be saved is not stated.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Asks first</dt><dd>Sending outreach, creating a draft in another system and writing to the CRM each need a separate, explicit request.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Will not</dt><dd>It does not invent company facts, partner terms, contacts, reputation or evidence of fit. Outreach stays a draft, and material claims carry a source.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Whose work it covers</dt><dd>A single company, partner or type of partner that you name, and the people you mean to reach there.</dd></div>
<div><dt>On a schedule</dt><dd>Not stated.</dd></div>
<div><dt>With no app connected</dt><dd>Nothing is required. It works from supplied material and public research, and labels assumptions and gaps.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Company context it uses</dt><dd>The positioning you have approved, your proof points, what makes a good partner and any limits on outreach.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Before and after</dt><dd>Once a conversation is live: <a class="skl" href="#gpt-plan-deal-strategy"><code>plan-deal-strategy</code></a> or <a class="skl" href="#gpt-prepare-for-meeting"><code>prepare-for-meeting</code></a>. Finding contacts belongs to <a class="skl" href="#gpt-enrich-company-and-contact-data"><code>enrich-company-and-contact-data</code></a>. Internal look-ups belong to <a class="skl" href="#gpt-find-key-internal-sources"><code>find-key-internal-sources</code></a>.</dd></div>
<div><dt>GTM job <small>Our read</small></dt><dd>Account research and call preparation</dd></div>
</dl>

<details class="gsk" id="gpt-analyze-account-signals">
<summary><span class="nm"><code>analyze-account-signals</code></span><span class="tg">What changed at an account</span><span class="fitm"><span class="t">Sellers</span><span class="t">Can be scheduled</span></span><span class="pm" aria-hidden="true"></span></summary>
<div class="body">
<p class="does">Tells you how a single account stands today, or sums up what has materially changed across a watchlist of limited size.</p>
<div class="gcard">
<div class="runhead"><h3>Use it</h3><p>As ChatGPT reported the skill's instructions. The example request is ours and was not run.</p></div>
<dl class="runkv">
<div><dt>When</dt><dd>A status check on a named account or a question about what changed there. Also a request for a digest of an owner&#x27;s portfolio or of a watchlist. Ranking a whole book belongs to the seller dashboard skill.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Ask</dt><dd><p class="sayline"><span class="sayt" id="gask-analyze-account-signals">@Sales what has changed at <span class="slot">[account]</span> in the last 14 days?</span><button class="btn sm jsonly" type="button" data-copy="gask-analyze-account-signals">Copy</button></p></dd></div>
<div><dt>Give it</dt><dd>One identifiable company or CRM account. For monitoring: a watchlist, or an owner it can identify. Optional: the time window, which defaults to 14 days, focus areas and the style of the output.</dd></div>
<div><dt>You get</dt><dd>For one account: a snapshot, recent signals, an interpretation, recommended actions, open questions and missing evidence. For a watchlist: accounts ranked by material change, an action for each, details of the run and a plain statement where nothing much moved.</dd></div>
</dl>
</div>
<dl class="kv">
<div><dt>Reads</dt><dd>The CRM for who owns the account, its health and status, open deals and recent activity. Call transcripts for the customer&#x27;s own words and decisions. Email for progress and requests left unanswered. Internal messages, account plans and notes for what the team knows. The calendar for meetings ahead.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Can create or change</dt><dd>Nothing: the readout changes no record. Follow-ups are CRM-ready updates drafted for review and an offer to set up a recurring monitor. A recurring run may not post a digest or write to the CRM by itself.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Asks first</dt><dd>No schedule until the user agrees. Tasks, posts, monitoring state kept elsewhere and CRM updates each need a separate request and approval.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Will not</dt><dd>It does not read a past change into a field&#x27;s current value or manufacture a trend. Given a portfolio that is too large, it does not pick part of it at random. Monitoring never becomes CRM updates, tasks or posts made without the user.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Whose work it covers</dt><dd>A single account, or a watchlist of 50 accounts at most. An owner can be picked by ID or by email. Nothing is said about a manager&#x27;s permission.</dd></div>
<div><dt>On a schedule</dt><dd>A daily or weekly rerun of the watchlist summary that only reads. It checks active and paused schedules for a duplicate first.</dd></div>
<div><dt>With no app connected</dt><dd>Complete records or an export that you supply. If it cannot establish the account or the change, it asks for the smallest missing identifier or piece of evidence.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Company context it uses</dt><dd>CRM ownership, territory, the portfolio or watchlist, account status and the focus areas a user defines.</dd></div>
<div><dt>Before and after</dt><dd>For a live deal: <a class="skl" href="#gpt-plan-deal-strategy"><code>plan-deal-strategy</code></a>. For a coming conversation: <a class="skl" href="#gpt-prepare-for-meeting"><code>prepare-for-meeting</code></a>.</dd></div>
<div><dt>GTM job <small>Our read</small></dt><dd>Account research and call preparation</dd></div>
</dl>
